That’s what I think of when you say on edge. Like a tightly wound spring that's about to snap. \n\nBut me? No, I don't have that feeling at all. Even with all the thoughts spinning around about the house and second-guessing my orders, it doesn't come out as a jittery thing. I'm not pacing around the breakroom or tapping my foot incessantly or feeling my heart pound out of my chest. It’s... it's really the exact opposite of being on edge. It's more like being at the bottom of a pool. Just this heavy, slow, hollow sensation I was telling you about. The worry about my job and everything is definitely there in my head, sure, but it just sits in my chest like a lead weight. It doesn't make me jumpy or nervous or anything like that. So, yeah, I wouldn't say I'm on edge most days. I'm not keyed up at all.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince these feelings began, have you noticed any change in your appetite or weight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"My eating has been all over the place, where some weeks I barely touch staff meal and other nights I come home and eat whatever’s in the kitchen just to feel settled.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Appetite? Yeah, actually, that's been really weird lately. My eating has just been completely all over the place. 

It’s strange because in the restaurant industry, you kind of live and die by the staff meal. You know, family meal? Usually around 4:00 PM, right before the dinner rush really starts hitting and the doors open for service, the prep cooks will throw something together for the front of house. Sometimes it's just leftover pasta bake or chicken scraps, but it's free food and you need the calories when you're walking five miles a night on hard tile floors. I’ve always been the guy who grabs a heaping plate and eats it standing up by the expo line. But lately... man, some weeks I barely touch staff meal at all. I'll look at a plate of whatever they made, and it just looks like a pile of cardboard. I have zero desire to eat. I'll just push it around with my fork and end up throwing it in the dish pit trash because my stomach just feels totally indifferent to it. I'll run an entire eight-hour shift on nothing but iced tea and maybe a dinner roll. 

But then, the flip side is just as bad. Other nights, I'll get home after one of those brutal closing shifts I was telling you about, and I'll walk into my house and it's like a switch flips. I won't even be physically hungry—not really—but I'll come home and just eat whatever's in the kitchen. I'll stand there in the dark with the fridge door open, eating cold leftovers, grabbing handfuls of dry cereal straight out of the box, maybe some stale tortilla chips... just mindlessly shoving it in. It's not because it tastes good. It's more like I'm doing it just to feel settled, you know? Like I'm trying to physically push down that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest by filling my stomach until it hurts. 

So because of this whole yo-yo routine, my weight is probably fluctuating a bit. I haven't actually stepped on a scale in the bathroom to check the exact numbers, but I can feel it in how my uniform fits. Some days my black work slacks feel loose because I've basically fasted for three days straight, and then other days the waistband is digging into me because I spent the last two nights scavenging through my pantry at 1 AM. It’s just completely erratic. I either want absolutely nothing, or I want to consume everything in the house just to calm myself down.

It's nice to be outside in the fresh air after being stuck inside the restaurant all week smelling like fry grease and dirty dishwater. \n\nSo, yeah, I still get a kick out of that. And I still like watching baseball on the weekends. My brother usually comes over, we order a pizza, watch the game, and I still look forward to that just as much as I ever did. It's not that I don't care about my hobbies anymore, or that I don't want to do them... it's more just that when I am doing them, that heavy feeling is still sitting right there on my chest. Like, I'll be watching the game, enjoying it, having a slice of pizza, but in the back of my mind, I'm just thinking about the mortgage and how I shouldn't be relaxing when I'm such a screw-up at work. But the actual enjoyment of the game itself? No, that's still there. I definitely still like doing my normal stuff. I just can't turn off the guilt while I'm doing it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy guilt and self-criticism?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and think about it, and trace it back... it's been a while. It's not just a recent thing, you know? I'd say it's been about, gosh, a year and two months now that I've been feeling this really intense, heavy depression and just this constant guilt. It actually started right around the time we first started seriously looking at houses, before we even closed on this one. \n\nI remember it was early spring last year, because we were going to all these open houses on my days off, and every time we'd walk through one, instead of feeling excited, I'd just get this pit in my stomach. I'd look at the price sheets the realtors hand out, those glossy little flyers, and my mind would just start racing about how I'm just a waiter, how I didn't finish my degree, and how I have no business taking on a quarter-million dollar loan. And it just stuck with me. \n\nSo yeah, for about a year and two months, it's just been this constant companion. Even when we finally found the house and got the keys, which should have been this huge, happy milestone, I just felt like a fraud. I remember standing in the empty living room on move-in day, holding a box of kitchen stuff, and just feeling like crying because I felt so guilty for putting my family in this position. And it hasn't let up since. It's just been this solid block of time, fourteen months or so, of feeling like I'm doing everything wrong and dragging everyone down with me. It’s exhausting, honestly, just carrying it around day in and day out for over a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about killing yourself or not wanting to be alive?\n\nPatient: Well, gosh, um... I want to be really clear right off the bat that I don't have any kind of plan to actually hurt myself. I'm not sitting around trying to figure out ways to do something drastic or anything like that. I really want to make sure you know that. But... if I'm being completely honest with you, and I guess that's why I'm here, there are definitely times when the idea of just not being here anymore crosses my mind. \n\nIt almost always happens when I'm scheduled for one of those really early opening shifts at the restaurant. I have to be in at, like, 5:30 AM to set up the coffee stations, make sure the registers are ready, and roll the silverware before the breakfast rush hits. And I'll be lying there in bed at maybe 4:00 or 4:30 in the morning, just staring up at the ceiling in the dark, listening to the house settle. My wife is usually fast asleep next to me, the whole neighborhood is dead quiet, but my brain is just already spinning out of control about the mortgage and how I'm probably going to mess up a bunch of orders as soon as I clock in. \n\nAnd in those moments, right before my alarm is supposed to go off, I just... I sometimes think it would be so much easier if I just didn't wake up. You know? If I could just stay asleep and not have to actually get out from under the covers and face another day of just... failing at everything. It’s not that I actively want to take my own life, it’s just that the thought of having to keep trying and keep making mistakes and keep feeling like this massive disappointment to everyone is just so incredibly exhausting. So, no, I don't want to kill myself, but that thought of just peacefully not waking up so I don't have to keep messing up... that definitely happens when I'm lying awake dreading those morning shifts.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different areas of your life, beyond the mortgage and your job?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that for a second. Um... no, not really. I mean, if I'm really looking at the big picture, it pretty much all just circles right back to the house and the restaurant. It’s funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day. Like, I don't really worry about my health, you know? I'm 38, I'm on my feet all day at work, carrying heavy trays and bus tubs, so I get plenty of exercise, and I don't sit around stressing about getting sick or my blood pressure or anything like that. \n\nAnd I don't really worry about my marriage, either, aside from the guilt I mentioned about feeling like I'm letting her down. But my wife and I, we're solid. We don't fight about little things, and I don't stress about where our relationship is going or if she's mad at me. \n\nEven with, like, normal day-to-day stuff... take my car, for example. I drive this older sedan, it's got like 140,000 miles on it, and the AC makes this awful squealing noise when you first turn it on. A lot of guys would be losing sleep over when the compressor is going to blow, or how they're going to pay for a mechanic, but I just... I don't really care? I just turn the radio up until it stops squeaking. It just doesn't register as something to panic about. \n\nSo, yeah, it’s not like I’m walking around terrified of everything in the world. It really is just this intense, hyper-focused thing on the mortgage and my job. Because, in my head, they're totally connected, right? If I screw up at the restaurant—which, like I said, I always feel like I'm doing—then I lose my good shifts, and if I lose the shifts, I can't pay the mortgage, and then we lose the house that we just bought. So it's like this one giant loop of stress about those two specific things. But outside of that little bubble? No, I wouldn't say I'm a worrier about, you know, the rest of the world or other parts of my life. It's just those two things taking up absolutely all the space in my head.\n\nDoctor: Apart from the guilt and worry, have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um, sad is... I mean, I guess sad is one word for it, but it's almost more than just being sad, or maybe less than that? It's hard to explain. Let me try to put it into words. If I really think about how my days actually feel, most days, almost every single day really, there's just this... this incredibly heavy, completely empty feeling right here in the middle of my chest. It's almost like a physical weight, you know? Like I swallowed a stone or a piece of lead, and it just sits there, right behind my ribs. \n\nAnd it's especially obvious when I'm at the restaurant. I'll be out on the floor, working my section, carrying these massive trays of plates—because we use those really thick, heavy ceramic plates at our place, the kind that look rustic but weigh an absolute ton when you have four or five of them balanced on your arm—and I'm just totally, completely going through the motions. I'll walk up to a four-top, and I do the whole routine, right? I say, \"Hi folks, welcome in, how are we doing today?\" And I'm smiling, I'm doing the big customer service smile, nodding at whatever joke the guy at the table makes about being hungry enough to eat a horse... but I'm only doing it because I'm supposed to. It's part of the uniform. \n\nIt honestly feels like I'm just acting the part of a waiter in a play. Because inside, behind that smile, I don't feel anything good at all. There's no joy in it, no actual warmth or connection with the people I'm serving. It’s just that heavy, hollow ache in my chest that stays with me from the minute I tie my apron on to the minute I finish my side-work and head out to my car. So, yeah, I guess \"down\" is a good way to put it, because it just feels like all the actual feeling has been drained out of me, and I'm just carrying plates and faking it all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your energy level since these feelings began?\n\nPatient: Energy... um, you know, it's funny you ask that, because with everything else going on, with that heavy feeling in my chest and all the worrying about the house, you'd probably think I'd be totally wiped out. Like, just dragging myself out of bed and barely able to put one foot in front of the other. But honestly? No, I wouldn't say my energy level has really changed at all. \n\nI'm still working all my normal shifts, and like I was saying before about those heavy ceramic plates, this job takes a lot out of you physically. A bunch of us at the restaurant actually track our steps on our phones—it's sort of a little competition we have going to see who walks the most on a busy Friday night—and I'm still hitting like 15,000, sometimes 18,000 steps a shift. I'm still hustling from the kitchen to the dining room, running out to the patio to check on tables, carrying those massive bus tubs back to the dish pit. And let me tell you, those tubs get incredibly heavy when people just pile their half-eaten food and all the silverware in them, but I'm still hoisting them up and hauling them back there just as fast as I always have. \n\nSo, physically, my body is still moving at the exact same speed. I'm not, you know, falling asleep on my breaks or needing to take long naps in the afternoon or anything like that. Even when I get home after a double shift, I'm still doing my chores, I'm still mowing the lawn, I'm still taking out the trash and doing whatever needs to be done around the house. It's just that I'm doing all of it while feeling completely hollow inside, like I was trying to explain earlier. The emotional part is exhausting, sure, but the actual physical energy to get up and do the work? No, I haven't noticed a drop in that. I'm still going, going, going all day long, just like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Since these feelings began, have you had trouble concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Concentrating... yeah, actually. That's... that's been a real problem lately. It's incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I've been doing this for years, right? I know the restaurant inside and out. But lately, when I go to ring in an order, I'm just having so much trouble keeping the numbers straight on the POS system. We have these touch screens at the server stations, and usually, my fingers just fly over them. It's muscle memory. But now, I'll take an order for a big table, walk over to the POS, and I'll just... freeze. I'll punch everything in, and then I'll just stand there and stare at the screen. I know I’ve done this a million times, but I just completely stop trusting what I just entered. I'll think, 'Wait, did seat three want the dressing on the side? Did I hit the button for medium-rare or medium-well?' And I just stare at it, totally second-guessing myself.\n\nAnd it happens with the inventory sheets, too. Sometimes the manager has me help out with the end-of-month counts in the liquor room or dry storage because I'm usually really reliable with that stuff. But lately, I'll be looking at the clipboard, counting bottles of well vodka or boxes of napkins, and I just lose my train of thought completely. I'll write a number down and immediately not trust it, so I have to count the same shelf three or four times. It's like my brain just won't hold onto the information for more than a second. \n\nIt's maddening, honestly, because it slows me down, and then I get anxious that the managers are going to notice I'm lagging, which of course just feeds right back into the whole worry about losing my job and the house that I was talking about before. So, physically I can still run around the dining room, but mentally? Just making a simple decision or trusting myself to read a screen feels like wading through mud right now. I'm just constantly staring at the monitor, wondering if I'm screwing everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep since these feelings began?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... no, actually. No change there. I mean, my sleep schedule has always been kind of chaotic just by the nature of the industry, you know how it is. In the restaurant business, your hours are all over the place. Sometimes the scheduling manager has me on a 'clopen'—which is what we call it when you close the restaurant at midnight and then have to be right back there to open the doors at 10 AM the next morning. It's a pretty brutal turnaround. But as far as my actual ability to sleep, or how much sleep I'm getting? No, that's all exactly the same as it's always been. \n\nUsually, if I'm on a closing shift, I'll get home around 11:30 or midnight, and my feet are just absolutely throbbing from all those steps I was telling you about earlier. I'll come in through the back door so I don't make too much noise, toss my apron in the wash, and take a really hot shower just to scrub off the smell of the kitchen. Because no matter what you do, even if you're just out on the floor all night, the smell of fryer grease and stale beer just seeps right into your pores. So I do that whole wind-down routine, and then honestly, the second my head hits the pillow... I'm out. I just crash. \n\nI sleep my solid six or seven hours, depending on when my next shift starts, just like I have for the last ten years. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up in the middle of the night staring at the ceiling or anything like that. It's actually kind of surprising, right? You'd think with all the staring at the POS screen and second-guessing myself, and that heavy, hollow feeling sitting in my chest all day, that my brain would just keep me up all night worrying about the house and my job and everything else. But I guess my body is just so physically conditioned to the routine of working a long shift and then passing out that it just overrides the mental stuff once I get into bed. So, yeah, my sleep hasn't been affected at all. I'm still sleeping like a rock.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel nervous or on edge most days because of these concerns?\n\nPatient: Nervous? On edge? Um... no, not really. I mean, I know I just said a minute ago that I get anxious about the managers noticing me lagging at the screen, but it's not... it's not like a physical nervousness, if that makes sense. \n\nWhen I hear the phrase \"on edge,\" I always picture, you know, a Friday night when the kitchen is totally in the weeds, ticket times are running forty-five minutes, and the expediter is just screaming for food runners. You see the newer servers, the ones who are maybe nineteen or twenty and haven't been in the industry very long, and they are literally shaking. They're dropping trays of water glasses, they're biting their nails, they're practically vibrating near the heat lamps because they're so keyed up and terrified of their tables yelling at them. That’s what I think of when you say on edge. Like a tightly wound spring that's about to snap. \n\nBut me? No, I don't have that feeling at all. Even with all the thoughts spinning around about the house and second-guessing my orders, it doesn't come out as a jittery thing. I'm not pacing around the breakroom or tapping my foot incessantly or feeling my heart pound out of my chest. It’s... it's really the exact opposite of being on edge. It's more like being at the bottom of a pool. Just this heavy, slow, hollow sensation I was telling you about. The worry about my job and everything is definitely there in my head, sure, but it just sits in my chest like a lead weight. It doesn't make me jumpy or nervous or anything like that. So, yeah, I wouldn't say I'm on edge most days. I'm not keyed up at all.\n\nDoctor: Since these feelings began, have you noticed any change in your appetite or weight?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Yeah, actually, that's been really weird lately. My eating has just been completely all over the place. \n\nIt’s strange because in the restaurant industry, you kind of live and die by the staff meal. You know, family meal? Usually around 4:00 PM, right before the dinner rush really starts hitting and the doors open for service, the prep cooks will throw something together for the front of house. Sometimes it's just leftover pasta bake or chicken scraps, but it's free food and you need the calories when you're walking five miles a night on hard tile floors. I’ve always been the guy who grabs a heaping plate and eats it standing up by the expo line. But lately... man, some weeks I barely touch staff meal at all. I'll look at a plate of whatever they made, and it just looks like a pile of cardboard. I have zero desire to eat. I'll just push it around with my fork and end up throwing it in the dish pit trash because my stomach just feels totally indifferent to it. I'll run an entire eight-hour shift on nothing but iced tea and maybe a dinner roll. \n\nBut then, the flip side is just as bad. Other nights, I'll get home after one of those brutal closing shifts I was telling you about, and I'll walk into my house and it's like a switch flips. I won't even be physically hungry—not really—but I'll come home and just eat whatever's in the kitchen. I'll stand there in the dark with the fridge door open, eating cold leftovers, grabbing handfuls of dry cereal straight out of the box, maybe some stale tortilla chips... just mindlessly shoving it in. It's not because it tastes good. It's more like I'm doing it just to feel settled, you know? Like I'm trying to physically push down that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest by filling my stomach until it hurts. \n\nSo because of this whole yo-yo routine, my weight is probably fluctuating a bit. I haven't actually stepped on a scale in the bathroom to check the exact numbers, but I can feel it in how my uniform fits. Some days my black work slacks feel loose because I've basically fasted for three days straight, and then other days the waistband is digging into me because I spent the last two nights scavenging through my pantry at 1 AM. It’s just completely erratic. I either want absolutely nothing, or I want to consume everything in the house just to calm myself down."
